MEMORANDUM DECISION AND ORDER 


This case is before the court on a summons and 
complaint filed by the various plaintiffs contending that 
defendant, the Sate of Wisconsin, violated the Establish- 
ment Clause of the First Amendment of the United States 
Constitution, Article I, sec. 18 of the Wisconsin Consti- 
tution, Article IV, sec. 18 (the “public or local bill” law) 
of the Wisconsin Constitution, Article X, sec. 3 (the 
“uniformity clause” law) of the Wisconsin Constitution, 
and the public purpose doctrine, by creating 1995 Wis. 
Act 27, secs. 4000-4010, amending sec. 119.23, Stats. 
(the Milwaukee Parental Choice Program). Plaintiffs 
seek declaratory and injunctive relief. Defendants con- 
tend the Milwaukee Parental Choice Program, as 
amended, is constitutional under the federal and Wis- 
consin constitutions. 


All parties have filed motions for summary judgment, 
stipulating that no material fact is in dispute and that 
they are entitled to judgment as a matter of law. This 
case is ripe for summary consideration from this court." 


Federal and state constitutional provisions are impli- 
cated in this case. It is prudent, however, for this court 
to rely on the state provisions if possible without answer- 
ing the federal questions. This court does so and does not 
address any of the federal questions posed by this case. 
Furthermore, plaintiffs NAACP, et al. raise Article X, 
sec. 1 objections to the program, which will not be ad- 
dressed. Finally, defendants/internors Marquelle Miller, 
et al. claim in defense that should this court strike down 


1 Plaintiffs NAACP filed their complaint in August, 1996, two 
days before oral arguments were heard on defendants’ motion to 
lift the preliminary injunction imposed by the Wisconsin Supreme 
Court. After written arguments, this court consolidated the 
NAACP complaint with this action but stayed their equal protec- 
tion claim. The balance of the NAACP’s complaint was allowed to 
proceed since their claims were essentially the same as the other 
plaintiffs’. 


140a 


the program, sec. 119.23, et seq., as amended, will violate 
the federal First Amendment and the freedom of religion 
or conscience provision of Article I, sec. 18. No specific 
reference is made to this defense since it is not properly 
before the court as a claim. And, as you will see, the 
entire program is found to be unconstitutional. 


This court has reviewed all briefs with attachments, 
given serious consideration to all oral arguments pre- 
sented, and has examined the law scrupulously. With all 
of this in mind and for the following reasons this court 
holds that the amended MPCP violates Article I, sec. 18, 
Article ITV, sec. 18 of the Wisconsin Constitution, and 
the public purpose doctrine as it is applied to sectarian 
schools. The amended MPCP does not violate Article X, 
sec. 3 of the Wisconsin Constitution and the public pur- 
pose doctrine as it is applied to nonsectarian schools. 
Therefore, the entire MPCP as amended is unconstitu- 
tional and is ordered to be terminated. However, based 
on stipulation by all parties, this order is stayed until the 
close of the 1996-97 school year for all private schools 
participating in the amended MPCP. 


Let us be clear about what is not at issue in this case. 
Much has been made about whether “school choice” is 
sound public policy. That concern is best left to the 
legislature. The scope of this court’s role is strictly con- 
fined to considerations of possible constitutional infringe- 
ment. Ours is to interpret and apply the law to facts as 
they are presented. Ours is not to second-guess the legis- 
lature and wear the mantel of policy makers. “School 
choice” may in fact be sound public policy, especially 
considering the sad plight of the Milwaukee Public School 
system. I refuse the urgings to consider policy arguments 
and, instead, place my sights on the legality of the 
MPCP, as amended. 


14la 


STANDARD OF REVIEW 


Defendants contend this challenge to the constiutionality 
of the amended MPCP is a facial challenge since the plain- 
tiffs brought this suit before the program was implemented. 
If that is the case, “(p)laintiffs must . . . ‘establish that 
no set of circumstances exists under which the [statute] 
would be valid.’” See United States v. Salerno, 481 
U.S. 739, 745 (1987). 


However, this court does not agree with the defendant's 
contention that a discussion of the specific application of 
the amended MPCP statute would merely be a matter of 
conjecture and speculation rather than established fact. 
The record is renlete with facts. The amended MPCP 
was implemented after the plaintiffs brought this case. 
This has given the court ample opportunity to assess the 
actual operations of the program. Furthermore, both 
sides have agreed to a stipulated set of facts regarding the 
program. Thus, this court must judge the constitutionality 
of the program based on the application of the amended 
MPCP statute, and the motions for summary judgment 
will be decided accordingly. 


BACKGROUND 


Plaintiff ACLU and defendant State of Wisconsin elo- 
quently lay out the background facts in their submissions 
to this court. Rather than attempt to restate what they 
so well have already stated, I incorporate relevant por- 
tions into this opinion with minor modifications. 


A. Milwaukee Schools: An Educational Crisis 


It is beyond dispute that the Milwaukee Public Schools 
(MPS) ‘face the most serious educational challenges in 
the State. Indeed, the Supreme Court of Wisconsin recog- 
nized just a few years ago that “the Milwaukee Public 
School District has significantly greater education and 
poverty problems than any other school district in the 
state.” Davis v. Grover, 166 Wis. 2d 501, 490 N.W.2d 


142a 


460, 469 (1992). Moreover, “[tJhe statistical data clearly 
illustrated that the socioeconomic disparities and the edu- 
cational problems are greater in the large urban area of 
Milwaukee than any other part of Wisconsin.” Id. at 
529-30; see also Kukor v. Grover, 148 Wis. 2d 469, 
482-83, 436 N.W.2d 568, 573-74 (1989). 


By any measure of educational performance, Milwaukee 
Public Schools have performed dismally. For example, in 
1989, when the original Choice Plan was under consid- 
eration, the dropout rate for high-school students reached 
14.4 percent. Davis at~529. At the same time, the 
schools in the rest of the state enjoyed the relatively low 
dropout rate of 3.11 percent. See id. at 529. In light 
of the dropout rates in the MPS, it is not surprising that 
in 1989 less than 45 percent of MPS students graduated 
from high school within six years—down from 57 percent 
just five years earlier. 7d. at 531. And of those who did 
graduate within six years in 1989, more than one third 
did so with a “D” average. Id. 


The Wisconsin Supreme Court in Davis v. Grover 
found that these realities “dramatically show the need for 
legislative attention.” Id .at 529. That legislative atten- 
tion began with the original Milwaukee Parental Choice 
Program (the “MPCP” or “Choice Program”) enacted in 
1989, and continued with the amended MPCP now under 
challenge. 1995 Wisconsin Act 27, §§ 4002-09 (the 
“amended MPCP”). 


B. The Original Choice Program 


In the spring of 1990, the Wisconsin Legislature created 
the Milwaukee Parental Choice Program, which is codified 
at sec. 119.23, Stats. The Original MPCP provided that 
“any pupil in grades kindergarten to 12 who resides 
within the city [of Milwaukee] may attend, at no charge, 
any nonsectarian private school located in the city,” as 
long as certain requirements were met. See Sec. 119.23(2) 
(a), Stats. (1993-94). 


143a 


From 1990 to 1994, the MPCP was open to 1% of 
the membership of the Milwaukee Public School district. 
For the 1994-95 school year, that percentage was raised to 
1.5%—approximately 1,460 students. See Sec. 119.23 
(2)(b)1., Stats. (1993-94); Agreed Upon Statement of 
Facts, 4 8. To take part in the program, a student’s family 
income cannot exceed 1.75 times the federal poverty level. 
See Sec. 119.23(2)(a)1., Stats. As originally enacted, 
no more than 49% of a private school’s total enrollment 
could consist of pupils attending under the MPCP. See 
Sec. 119.23(2)(b)2., Stats. (1989-90). That percentage 
was increased to 65 percent by 1993 Wis. Act 16, sec. 
2300. 


Under the Original MPCP, the Superintendent of 
Public Instructic - paid participating private schools an 
amount equal to the per-pupil student aid provided to the 
Milwaukee public schools. See Sec. 119.23(4), Stats. 
(1993-94). The moneys disbursed under the MPCP are 
appropriated from general purpose revenues, see secs. 
20.005(3) (fu) and 119.23(4), Stats., which consist pri- 
marily of general taxes collected by the state. See sec. 
20.001, Stats. 


C. The Amended Choice Program 


The 1995-96 Budget Act, 1995 Wis. Act 27, which 
Governor Thompson signed into law July 26, 1995, ex- 
panded the MPCP. Most significantly, the Act eliminated 
the prohibition on participation by religious schools in the 
MPCP:

3. of the statutes are amended to read: 

119.23(2)(intro.) Subject to par. (b), beginning 
in the 1990-91 school year, any pupil in grades 
kindergarten to 12 who resides within the city may 


attend, at no charge, any [nonsectarian]* private 
school located in the city if all of the following

The Budget Act also enlarged the number of students 
who can participate in the MPCP. As many as 7% of 
the Milwaukee school district’s pupil population would 
have been able to participate in the Program during the 
1995-96 school year, and up to 15 percent of the student 
population are eligible beginning in 1996-97. See id., 
sec. 4003, repealing and recreating sec. 119.23(2)(b), 
Stats. Based on the 1995-96 Milwaukee School District 
estimated pupil population of 98,370 students (see 
Agreed Upon Statement of Facts, 412) nearly 7,000 
students could have participated in the expanded MPCP 
in the 1995-96 school year, with up to 15,000 students 
potentially taking part in later years. The Act also re- 
pealed the restriction in the Original MPCP limiting a 
private school’s enrollment to 65 percent MPCP students. 
See 1995 Wis. Act 27, sec. 4003. 


The Budget Act amended the method by which the 
Superintendent pays tuition to private schools participating 
in the MPCP. Rather than making the check payable to 
the school as under the Original MPCP, the Superintendent 
will now make checks payable to the pupil’s parent or 
guardian. The checks are sent not to the parents, how-

must “restrictively endorse the check for the use~of the

There are no limitations on how private schools, includ- 
ing religious schools, may use the funds they receive under 
the MPCP. The funds may be used to support all of the 
educational functions of the school, including religious 
education, and they may be used to maintain, repair or 
construct facilities, including facilities used for religious 
purposes. See Agreed Upon Statement of Facts, § 28. 
As under the Original MPCP, funds paid to schools under 
the Amended MPCP are drawn from general purpose 
revenues. See sec. 119.23(4), Stats., as amended by 1995 
Wis. Act 27, sec. 4006m. 


Private schools that participate in the Amended MPC 
will receive the lesser of the amount of per-pupil state aid 
to the Milwaukee public schools under sec. 121.08, Stats. 
or “an amount equal to the private school’s operating and 
debt service cost per pupil that is related to educational 
programming, as determined by the department.” Jd. The 
per-pupil state aid provided to MPS under sec. 121.08 for 
the 1995-96 school year was approximately $3,667. See 
Agreed Upon Statement of Facts, § 29. For the 1996-97 


146a 


school year, the amount is approximately $4,400. See 
Deposition of Deputy State Superintendent of Public In- 
struction Steven B. Dold, p. 35, includec' in the Appendix 
to MTEA’s Motion for Summary Judgment. 


Under the Amended MPCP, participating schools must 
allow students to opt-out of “religious activities.” The 
amended siatute provides that: 


A private school may not require a pupil attending 
the private school under this section to participate 
in any religious activity if the pupil’s parent or guard- 
ian submits to the pupil’s teacher of the private 
school’s principal a written request that the pupil be 
exempt from such activities. 


Sec. 119.23(7)(c), Stats., created by 1995 Act 27, 
sec. 4008. Students attending a religious school under the 
MPCP who have not opted-out may participate, at state 
expense, in the full-range of religious activities offered by 
the school. rg 


Furthermore, the amended MPCP eliminated some of 
the reporting requirements included in the original MPCP. 
Under sec. 119.23(5)(d), Stats., of the original MPCP, 
the State Superintendent of Public Instruction was required 
to prepare and circulate a report to the relevant legislative 
committees each year. This report compared students par- 
ticipating in the MPCP with students enrolled in Mil- 
waukee public schools in the following areas: “academic 
achievement, daily attendance record, percentage of pupils 
suspended and expelled and parental involvement activities 
of pupils attending a private school.” Jd. The amended 
MPCP eliminated this requirement. The amended MPCP 
also eliminated sec. 119.23(9)(a), Stats., which author- 
ized the superintendent to “conduct one or more financial 
or performance evaluations, audits, or both.” 


The amended MPCP did maintain some of the reporting 
requirements, however. The participating schools are still 


1474 


required to follow uniform financial accounting standards, 
and must submit to annual independent financial audits 
under sec. 119.23(7)(am), Stats. In addition, sec. 119.23 
(9), Stats., requires the legislative audit bureau to per- 
form a financial and performance evaluation audit of the 
program by January 15, 2000 which will be distributed 
to the chief clerk of each house of the legislature and to 
appropriate standing committees.

religious schools. /d., 4 26.

DISCUSSION 


Article I, Sec. 18 of the Wisconsin Constitution 


Plainttiffs’ first claim the amended MPCP violates Art. 
1, sec. 18 of the Wisconsin Constitution, which provides 
as follows: 


The right of every person to worship Almighty God 
according to the dictates of conscience shall never 
be infringed; nor shall any person be compelled to 
attend, erect or support any place of worship, or to 
maintain any ministry, without consent; nor shall any 
control of, or interference with, the rights of con- 
science be permitted, or any preference be given by 
law to any religious establishment, or modes of wor- 
ship; nor shall any money be drawn from the treasury 
for the benefit of religious societies, or religious or 
theological seminaries. 


Plaintiffs’ claim focuses on two provisions: “. . . nor shall 
any person be compelled to attend, erect or support any 
place of worship, or to maintain any ministry without con- 
sent;” and “. . . nor shall any money be drawn from the 


costs ($22,489.78 last year) ; the cost of all utilities, ($38,- 
379.10 last year) ; building and grounds liability insurance, 
inspection fees, and licenses, ($11,370.76 last year); and 
the services of all parish based personnel, namely, the 
pastor, pastoral associate, parish secretary, and building 
engineer. 


3. St. Bernadette Church provides a yearly cash subsidy to 
assist the day school program with school based expenses 
... not to exceed 25% of the total cost of all school based 
expenses. Last year the cash subsidy in behalf of the day 
school program was $66,140.36.” 


154a 


treasury for the benefit of religious societies, or religious 
or theological seminaries.” These two provisions are com- 
monly referred to as the “compelled support” clause and 
the “benefit” clause, respectiveiy. Each clause will be dis- 
cussed in turn. 


A. The Benefit Clause 


The proper analysis for determining whether the 
amended MPCP is constitutional as to the sectarian 
schools is set forth in the seminal United States Supreme 
Court decision of Lemon v. Kurtzman, 403 U.S. 602 
(1971): The statute must have a secular legislative pur- 
pose; its primary effect must neither advance nor inhibit 
religion; and it must not foster excessive governmental 
entanglement with religion. 7d. at 612-13. See also, Tilton 
v. Richardson, 403 U.S. 672 (1971); State ex rel. Warren 
v. Nusbaum, 55 Wis. 2d 316, 198 N.W.2d 650, (1972); 
State ex rel. Wisconsin Health Facilities Authority v. 
Lindner, 91 Wis. 2d 145, 280 N.W.2d 773 (1979). Our 
concern in this case is whether the primary effect of includ- 
ing sectarian schools in the amended MPCP advances 
religion. I conclude that it does. 


In an attempt to give some organization to the Wiscon- 
sin cases applying the Lemon test to Article I, Sec: 18 
controversies, I provide the following analytical frame- 
work. We look to three factors to determine whether the 
amended MPCP meets the “primary effect” prong of the 
Lemon test as it is applied in Wisconsin: 


1. Does the legislation provide aid to an institution in 
which religion is so pervasive that a substantial portion of 
its functions are subsumed in the religious mission or it 
funds a specifically religious activity in an otherwise sub- 
stantially secular setting*. State ex rel. Warren v. Nus- 
baum, 64 Wis. 2d 314, 325, 219 N.W.2d 577 (1974); 


3A possible 4th factor exists, neutrality, which is part of the 
U.S. Supreme Court’s jurisprudence in Establishment Clause claims. 


155a 


2. Does the legislation provide for direct governmental 
aid to a sectarian institution. State ex rel. Reynolds v. 
Nusbaum, 17 Wis. 2d 148, 115 N.W.2d 761 (1962); and 


3. Who are the primary beneficiaries of this funding. 
Id. 


Each factor will be discussed in turn. However, before 
we do that, it is necessary to clarify the scope of this 
court’s analysis of Article I, Sec. 18 in light of federal 
court decisions interpreting the Establishment Clause of 
the First Amendment. 


At issue is whether the language of Article I, Sec. 18 
provides an independent and more prohibitive basis for 
review of “benefit” clause and “compelling support” clause 
claims than is provided by federal jurisprudence of the 
Establishment Clause of the First Amendment. Based on 
a review of the historical context within which Article I, 
Sec. 18 was created and how it has been interpreted in the 
past 107 years I conclude it does. 


A usual starting point for this type of discussion is a 
review of the legislative history to determine the intent of 
the framers. As noted by Chief Justice Heffernan in his 
dissenting opinion in King v. Village of Waunakee, 185 
Wis. 2d 25, 64, 517 N.W. 2d 671 (1994), the record of 
the 1847 constitutional convention provides little if any 
insight into the framers’ intent when they created Article 
I, Sec. 18. See, 4 Constitutional Series: The Attainment 
of Statehood 228, 312, 334, 713-5 (Milo M. Quaife ed. 


The Supreme Court employs this analysis separate from the Lemon 
test. Defendants argue the application of the neutrality analysis 
to Article I, sec. 18 because they believe Wisconsin courts are bound 
by all aspects of the U.S. Supreme Court’s Establishment Clause 
analysis when interpreting Article I, sec. 18. As discussed below, 
this court rejects defendants’ argument and does not apply the 
neutral legislation analysis to the facts of this case. Furthermore, 
no Wisconsin court has applied the neutrality analysis to Article 
I, sec. 18 claims. 


156a 


1928). We must, therefore, look for an early case where 
the Wisconsin Supreme Court interpreted and applied Arti- 
cle I, Sec. 18. 


State ex rel. Weiss v. District Board, 76 Wis. 177, 44 
N.W. 967 (1890) served as the Wisconsin Supreme 
Court’s opportunity to investigate the historical context 
within which Article I, Sec. 18 was created. Weiss also 
provides us with a first glimpse at how the pioneers of 
our courts construed this important constitutional pro- 
vision. 


At issue in Weiss was whether reading a Protestant 
Bible in the Edgerton schools constituted sectarian instruc- 
tion in violation of Article X, sec. 3 and whether it in- 
fringed upon the right of Catholics the free exercise of 
their conscience in violation of Article I, Sec. 18. The 
Weiss court explained rather elaborately the historical con- 
text of the creation of Article I, sec. 18: 


The relations of church and state have been the sub- 
ject of discussion for many centuries; and at certain 
times, and in certain nations of Europe, one particu- 
lar sect has been the established church of the state, 
and at other times or in other nations the belief of 
some other sect has been the established religion— 
while other sects, not so favored, were either extermi- 
nated altogether or permitted to remain on conditions 
more or less disagreeable and humiliating. These dis- 
criminations naturally generated bitternerss, enmities, 
and even cruel war among brethren. Many of the 
early immigrants to this country had felt the despot- 
ism of such intolerance, and came hither in conse- 
quence of it. They came from different countries of 
Europe, and, consequently, had experienced different 
types of intolerance. Some of them were as narrow 
minded in such matters as their oppressors had been; 
and hence no sooner acquired civil power than they 


157a 


themselves became intolerant towards all sects except 
their own. ... 


Wisconsin, as one of the later states admitted into 
the Union, having before it the experience of oihers, 
and probably in view of its heterogeneous population, 
. . . has in her organic law, probably furnished a 
more complete bar to any preference for, or discrimi- 
nation against, any religious sect, organization, or 
society than any other state in the Union. 


Id. at 205, 207-208. It is undoubtedly clear that the 
Weiss court was signaling to all concerned that in response 
to Wisconsin’s unique history, the constitutional framers 
intended to provide strict protection to Wisconsin citizens 
in the exercise of their religious beliefs while at the same 
time placing great restrictions on the state with respect to 
compelling its citizens to support “any place of worship” 
and drawing funds from the treasury “for the benefit of 
religious societies, or religious or theological seminaries.” 
Id. at 211-216. 


The Court ruled that reading the Bible in public schools 
is “worship” within the meaning of the “compelling sup- 
port” clause. Jd. at 213. The Court further ruled that the 
school room in which the Bible is read is a “place of wor- 
ship.” Id. 


The Weiss court further ruled that religious instruction 
in a public school renders that school a “religious semi- 
nary” within the meaning of Article I, Sec. 18. The 
Edgerton school district received state funds and because 
of that the Court found that “the money so drawn from 
the state treasury was for the benefit of religious schools,” 
in violation of Article I, Sec. 18. Id. at 215. 


The Wisconsin Supreme Court elaborated on Weiss in 
the case of State ex rel. Reynolds v. Nusbaum, 17 Wis. 2d 
148, 115 N.W. 2d 761 (1962). In Reynolds plaintiffs 
claimed the state violated Article I, Sec. 18, the “benefits” 


158a 


clause, when it enacted a statute requiring school boards to 
provide private school students with free transportation to 
and from the nearest public school. Following in the foot- 
steps of Weiss, the Reynolds court construed “religious 
societies” to be synonymous with “religious organizations,” 
and that the term “seminary” includes elementary and sec- 
ondary schools. Jd. at 156, (cites omitted). The Court 
concluded that the private schools involved were religious 
organizations and therefore “religious seminaries” within 
the meaning of Article I, Sec. 18. Finding that the private 
schools benefit from the state providing State funded trans- 
portation to private parochial school students, the statute 
was held to violate Article I, Sec. 18. 


The Reynolds court was cognizant of the earlier U.S. 
Supreme Court decision in Everson v. Board of Education, 
330 U.S. 1 (1947), where the Court upheld a New Jersey 
law permitting parental reimbursement for the cost to bus 
children to parochial schools, finding no violation of the 
Establishment Clause of the First Amendment. The Reyn- 
olds court rejected an argument by the Wisconsin At- 
torney General urging it to adopt the Everson analysis and 
apply it to Article I, Sec. 18, quoting from Weiss and 
holding “that the First amendment provision, which pro- 
hibits laws’ respecting an establishment of religion,’ lends 
itself to more flexibility of interpretation than the provision 
contained in the last clause of Sec. 18, Art. I, of the Wis- 
consin Constitution.” Jd. at 165. 


The import of Reynolds lies in its clear pronouncement 
that Article I, sec. 18 provides greater limitations on the 
state’s authority when providing aid to private religious 
schools than the First Amendment. This part of the 


Reynolds opinion has not been overruled by any subse- 
quent decision.* 


* Defendants argue Reynolds has been repudiated as reflected by 
the evolution in Establishment Clause and “benefit” clause juris- 
prudence. No such clear pronouncement is articulated in any Wis-
