In State ex rel. Warren v. Reuter, 44 Wis. 2d 201, 170 
N.W. 2d 790 (1969), the Court adopted the primary 
effect test which it noted was first announced in Everson, 
330 U.S. 1. Id. at 227. The Court rejected an argument 
by the respondent that Reynolds rejected the primary effect 
test, explaining, “(g)ranting that Sec. 18, Art. I, of the 
Wisconsin Constitution is more prohibitive than the First 
Amendment of the federal constitution, it does not follow 
and we cannot read Sec. 18 as being so prohibitive as not 
to encompass the primary—effect test.” Jd. 


This is the first case where the Court acknowledges the 
more prohibitive limitations of Article I, sec. 18 as Op- 
posed to the First Amendment, while adopting the primary 
effect test. Obviously, the Warren court found the two 
concepts congenial, as not in conflict with the other. 
Lemon had yet to be decided. Still, in Wisconsin it was 
clear that Article I, sec. 18, even when applying the 
primary effect test to it, afforded greater prohibitions 
than the First Amendment against state funding of private 
religious institutions. 


The Lemon decision was handed down by the United 
States Supreme Court in 1971, as well as Tilton v. Rich- 
ardson, 403 U.S. 672 (1971), wherein the “Lemon” test 
was adopted. The Wisconsin Supreme Court in State ex 
rel. Warren v. Nusbaum, 55 Wis. 2d 316, 198 N.W. 2d 
650 (1972) (“Nusbaum I”) had its first full opportunity 
to comment on and apply the Lemon test to First Amend- 
ment claims. The court noted: “We are bound by the 
results and interpretation given the first amendment in 
these high court decisions (referring to Tilton and 
Lemon). Ours not to reason why; ours but to review and 
apply. That limited assignment is not made easier by 
apparent shifts in emphasis and differences between major- 


consin “benefit” clause or “compelled support” clause opinion. 


Hence, Reynolds is still good law in Wisconsin on this important 
point. 


160a 


ity, plurality, concurring and dissenting opinions.” Id. 
at 322. 


In “Nusbaum I” a statuate was challenged which au- 
thorized a contractual relationship between the state and 
the School of Dentistry of Marquette University for the 
providing of dental education in the state’s only dental 
school. The statute authorizing the contract placed no 
limitations on how the state funds were to be used. Some 
of the funds would go to Marquette University for its 
operating expenses, which conceivably could include ex- 
penditures made in support of its religious mission. A 
claim was brought under the First Amendment and 
Article I, Sec. 18. 


The primary effect test was applied in “Nusbaum I” 
and the statute at issue was found unconstitutional. The 
court first recognized the “dual purpose” nature of the 
First Amendment and Article I, Sec. 18 religious pro- 
visions: prohibiting the “establishment” of religion and 
protecting the “free exercise” of religion. The court 
found the statute in the first instance, unconstitutional 
under the First Amendment, and hence unconstitutional 
under Article I, Sec. 18. No thorough discussion was 
undertaken concerning the stronger prohibitions of Ar- 
ticle I, Sec. 18, nor did the Court employ a separate 
analysis under Article I, Sec. 18 as did the Court in 
Reynolds and Weiss. The court merely reiterated the 
accepted rule that “The crucial question . . . is not 
whether some benefit accrues to a religious institution as 
a consequence of the legislative program, but whether its 
principal or primary effect advances religion. Id. at 333, 
quoting from Tilton, Supra at 679. 


In State ex rel. Warren v. Nusbaum, 64 Wis. 2d 314, 
219 N.W. 2d 577 (1974) (“Nusbaum II”), the court 
upheld the constitutionality of a law that required school 
districts to contract with private schools offering special 
education services to children with exceptional needs if 
those services could not be provided by public schools 


16la 


or agencies. One significant restriction in the law, which 
is notably absent from the amended MPCP, was the 
governing board, faculty, student body and teachings of 
the private special education agency could not be “chosen 
or determined by any religious organization or for any 
sectarian purpose.” Jd. at 321. The court, referring to 
the secular nature of the service, held that the primary 
effect of the legislation was not to benefit religious or- 
ganizations. Jd. at 325-26. 


Similar to Nusbaum I, the court in Nusbaum II applied 
the primary effect test first under the federal constitution. 
Having found no violation under the federal constitution 
the court had no difficulty finding no violation under the 
Wisconsin Constitution. Again, no independent analysis 
was undertaken of the more strict prohibitions of Article 
I, sec. 18. However, the Nusbaum II court was the first 
court in Wisconsin to state the following proposition: 


“Aid may normally be thought of as having the 
primary effect of advancing religion when it flows 
to an institution in which religion is so pervasive 
that a substantial portion of its functions are sub- 
sumed in the religious mission . . .” 


Id. at 325. 


What follows from Nusbaum I and Nusbaum II are 
a line of cases involving the “benefits” clause where the 
court defers to federal jurisprudence generally, not exer- 
cising its right to determine the constitutionality of laws 
under the more strict provisions of Article I, Sec. 18, 
independent of the First Amendment. In these cases 
the Court simply applies Establishment Clause-primary 
jurisprudence to Article I, sec. 18 claims after first finding 
no violation of the federal constitution. The Court does 
not specifically, or implictly, reject the independent analy- 
sis of Article I, sec. 18 jurisprudence, as supported by 
Weiss, Reynolds, and Warren. See, State ex el. Holt vy. 
Thompson, 66 Wis. 2d 659, 225 N.W. 2d 678 (1975) 
(a writ of mandamus petition challenging the constitu- 


162a 


tionality of the “released time for religious instruction” 
act); State ex rel. Wisconsin Health Facilities Authority 
v. Lindner, 91 Wis. 2d 145, 280 N.W. 2d 773 (1979) 
(involving an act creating the “Authority” which finances 
improvements for private, nonprofit health care facilities 
through the sale of tax-exempt bonds; the primary effect 
of the act under the federal and Wisconsin constitutions 
was to provide aid to predominantly secular aspects of 
health care); Freedom From Religion Foundation, Inc. 
v. Thompson, 164 Wis. 2d 736, 476 N.W. 2d 318 (Ct. 
App. 1991) (an action challenging a legislative grant to 
St. Norbert College); King v. Village of Waunakee, 185 
Wis. 2d 25 (1994) (an attack on a Christmas creche 
located on government property and maintained at govern- 
ment expense). However, in State v. Miller, 202 Wis. 2d 
56, 549 N.W. 2d 235 (1996), the court reestablished in 
a firm and unequivocal way, the old rule established in 
Weiss and Reynolds that as to cases invoking the freedom 
of conscience provision of Article I, Sec. 18, its analysis, 
“is not constrained by the boundaries of protection the 
United States Supreme Court has set for the federal pro- 
vision.” Jd. at 66. The court held “that our state con- 
stitution provides an independent basis on which to decide 
this case.” Id. 


In Miller the court explained: 


Nowhere in King did we hold that Article I, sec. 18 
is subsumed by the First Amendment. Although we 
quoted with approval the court of appeals’ comment 
that because both clauses serve the same dual purpose 
we will interpret our provision “in light of United 
States Supreme Court cases,” that statement should 
not be read as an abandonment of our long-standing 
recognition that the language of the two documents 
is not the same. Some questions cannot be fully illu- 
minated by the light of federal jurisprudence alone, 
but may require examination according to the dictates 


1634 


of the more expansive protections envisioned by our 
State constitution. 


Id. at 63-64. 


The freedom of conscience or religion provision of Ar- 
ticle I, sec. 18 was the primary issue in Miller. Defend- 
ants argue a narrow interpretation of Miller, stating the 
Court’s reiteration of the increased protections of Article 
I, sec. 18 are confined to freedom of conscience claims. 
A careful reading of Miller and its reasoning leads this 
court to a different conclusion. 


The Court in Miller firmly repudiates any attempt by 
legal scholars and practitioners to read into King precisely 
what defendants are urging upon this court. The Court 
explains: 


Contrary to the reading ascribed to the King majority 
by both the dissent in that opinion (King, 185 Wis. 
2d at 59-60) and the court of appeals in this instance 
(Miller, 196 Wis. 2d at 245), we did not repudiate 
a reading of the Wisconsin Constitution which pro- 
vides stronger protection of religious freedom than 
that envisioned in the federal constitution. (footnote 
omitted). In fact, we explicitly stated that we reached 
our conclusion given the specific facts in King, “even 
assuming that” our state establishment provision 
might be “ ‘less flexible’ than the First Amendment.” 
(cites omitted). 


Id. at 64. Article I, sec. 18 was implicated in King as well 
as the Establishment Clause of the First Amendment. It 
is clear that the Court in King, which the Miller Court 
clearly acknowledged, examined Article I, sec. 18 and 
determined that its more rigid protections against govern- 
ment support of religion did not preclude the erection and 
maintenance of a christian creche on government property. 
This is a clear signal that this Court still recognizes the 
“less flexible” nature of Article I, sec. 18 in relation to the 


164a 


Establishment Clause of the First Amendment, and sup- 
ports an independent analysis of “benefit” clause and 


“compelled support” clause claims under the Wisconsin 
Constitution. 


We have every reason to extend the courts reasoning in 
Miller to all provisions contained in Article I, Sec. 18. 
First, those parts in Weiss and Reynolds finding and apply- 
ing the greater restrictions in “benefits” clause and “com- 
pelled support” clause cases have not been overruled, im- 
plicitly nor explicitly. Secondly, the “state’s unique history” 
as recognized by the drafters of our constitution applies 
equally to all portions of Article I, Sec. 18. Third, con- 
sistent with the need to evolve with changing times, we 
have observed in the past four to eight years a major 
effort to empower the states to determine their own des- 
tiny. Wisconsin has a strong constitution that has provided 
through time greater protections to individuals in the exer- 
cise of their religion while imposing clear constraints on 
the state’s authority to provide financial aid to sectarian 
schools, agencies and organization. 


I conclude, therefore, that there is an independent basis 
to analyze “benefit” clause and “compelled support” clause 
claims under Article I, sec. 18. While it is fruitful and 
prudent to interpret this provision “in light of United States 
Supreme Court cases,” we are not bound by federal juris- 
prudence when analyzing our own constitutional provi- 
sions. We now move to an independent analysis of the 
ameded MPCP under Article I, sec. 18. 


Factor 1: Does the legislation provide aid to an institu- 
tion in which religion is so pervasive that a substantial por- 
tion of its functions are subsumed in the religious mission 
or it funds a specifically religious activity in an otherwise 
substantially secular setting? The answer is yes. 


In Weiss and Reynolds the Court stated elementary and 
secondary schools providing religious or sectarian instruc- 


NT NT 


b65a 


tion are “religious seminaries” as that term is used in Ar- 
ticle I, sec. 18. There is no dispute that the sectarian 
schools wishing to participate or which currently partici- 
pate in the amended MPCP are “religious seminaries.” 
There is also no dispute that the amended MPCP would 
provide state funds from the state treasury to those schools 
should the program be allowed to continue. The question 
before us is can these schools separate the secular activity 
of education from the nonsecular activity reflected in their 
religious missions. 


Wisconsin and federal courts have generally acknowi- 
edged that elementary and secondary schools with a reli- 
gious affiliation are pervasively sectarian. See State ex rel. 
Wisconsin Health Facilities Authority v. Lindner, 91 Wis. 
2d 145, 156-57, See also, Meek v. Pittenger, 421 U.S. 349 
(1975); Committee for Public Education v. Nyquist, 413 
U.S. 756 (1973); Levitt v. Committee for Public Educa- 
tion, 413 U.S. 472 (1973). “The basis of this distinction 
is the recognition that the very purpose of many elemen- 
tary and secondary schools is to provide an integrated 
secular and religious education. The schools are often de- 
voted to the inculcation of religious values. Wisconsin 
Health Facilities Authority, at 156-57, citing Meek, 421 
US. at 366. 


In the first instance, we can assume as a matter of law 
that the elementary and secondary schools wishing to par- 
ticipate in the amended MPCP are pervasively sectarian. 
Wisconsin Health Facilities Authority, at 156-57. Then. 
when we review the stated missions of these schools as 
provided in the stipulated facts, their intent to inculcate 
their students with their religious doctrine is manifest. It 
is equally clear that it is impossible, under any scenario, 
for these schools to separate the secular function of edu- 
cation from their religious mission. Their very existence 
is to convert students to their religion and to proselytize 
their doctrine. We can easily conclude that the amended 


166a 


MPCP would provide aid to institutions in which religion 
is so pervasive that a substantial portion of their functions 
are subsumed in their religious mission. 


Factor 2: Does the legislation provide for direct gov- 
ernmental aid to a sectarian institution? The answer is 
yes. 


This is perhaps the most controversial of the issues pre- 
sented in this case. Plaintiffs argue that although the 
amended MPCP provides for “indirect” payments to the 
schools, direct government aid is still being provided to the 
religious schools. Defendants point to a long line of fed- 
eral cases that stand for the proposition that government 
payments to parents to subsidize their child’s education is 
“indirect” aid to the schools, thus not violative of the Es- 
tablishment Clause.® This conclusion is buttressed by the 
ability of the parents to independently choose the schools 
at which their child will attend. In this case the parents 
choose the school and inform the state Superintendent. 
The state Superintendent sends a check directly to the 
school which must be restrictly endorsed by both the 
school and the parents. 


It can hardly be said that this does not constitute direct 
aid to the sectarian schools. Although the U.S. Supreme 
Court has chosen to turn its head and ignore the real im- 
pact of such aid, this court refuses to accept that myth. 
Millions of dollars would be directed to religious institu- 
tions that are pervasively sectarian with a clear mission to 
indoctrinate Wisconsin students with their religious beliefs. 
Whether sent directly to the schools or sent directly to the 
schools with a mandate of restrictive endorsement by the 
parents, is irrelevant under Article I, sec. 18 which makes 


5 See Rosenberger v. Rector & Visitors of Univ. of Virginia, 115 
S.Ct. 2510 (1995); Zobrest v. Catalina Foothilss Sch. Dist., 113 
S.Ct. 2462 (1993); Witters v. Washington Dept. of Servs. for the 


Blind, 474 U.S. 481 (1986); and Muller v. Allen, 463 U.S. 388 
(1983). 


167a 


no distinction as to how the “benefit” is provided. As 
Stated earlier in this case, the state cannot do indirectly 
what it cannot do directly. And that is provide money 
from the state treasury to pervasively sectarian religious 
schools for the purpose of educating Wisconsin students. 


Factor 3: Who are the primary beneficiaries of this 
funding? 


The religious schools would be the primary beneficiaries 
of the government aid. The parents of students attending 
these schools will certainly gain a benefit. Indeed, such aid 
provides an inducement to the parents to send their chil- 
dren to the religious schools. However, the religious 
schools, which constitute approximately 89% of the pri- 
vate schools in the city of Milwaukee, would greatly bene- 
fit from the amended MPCP. See Agreed Upon Statement 
of Facts, 425. Millions of dollars would pour into their 
coffers, greatly enriching their ability to fulfill their reli- 
gious missions. 


In addition, state payments to participating religious 
schools under the amended MPCP are not limited to the 
tuition actually charged by the schools. The parties have 
stipulated that the tuition charged by a majority of the 
religious schools is less than the amount of state payment 
they would receive under the amended MPCP. See Agreed 
Upon Statement of Facts, § 30. The amended MPCP 
places no limitations on how the money may be used, in- 
cluding teachers salaries and operating expenses. Reynolds 
and Warren forbid the use of government funds for oper- 
ating expenses by religious institutions. Every dollar paid 
by the government exceeding the actual tuition provides 
a direct and substantial benefit to the religious schools. 


B. The Compelled Support Clause 


Article I, sec. 18 reads in pertinent part: “. . . nor shall 
any person be compelled to . . . support any place of wor- 


168a 


ship or to maintain any ministry, without consent. . . 
The amended MPCP violates the compelled support clause 
because it compels Wisconsin taxpayers to support reli- 
gious schools providing pervasively sectarian instruction 
rendering them places of worship and to maintain minis- 
tries without their consent. 


We have already discussed how the Court held in Weiss, 
supra, that Bible readings in public schools violates the 
“benefit” and the “compelled support” clauses. The Court 
found reading a Bible in public schools to constitute “wor- 
ship” and by permitting this activity in the schools, the 
schools became “place(s) of worship” within the mean- 
ing of Article I, sec. 18. Weiss, 76 Wis. at 211-13. Bible 
reading in the public schools violates the compelled sup- 
port clause because taxpayers of the school district with 
different religious beliefs were “compelled to aid in the 
erection of the school building in question, and also to aid 
in the support of the school maintained therein.” Jd. 
“Being thus compelled to aid in such erection and support, 
they have a legal right to object to its being used as a 
‘place of worship.’” Id. 


The amended MPCP also compels support of places of 
worship without the consent of taxpayers. Religious ele- 
mentary and secondary schools are pervasively sectarian, 
usually including religious worship and teachings in their 
institutions. There can be no question that the parochial 
schools are places of worship. 


” 


Tax dollars will be used to support these schools. As 
stated earlier, no limitations are placed on the use of the 
government aid. The schools may use these funds as oper- 
ating expenses, to purchase religious writing for intsruc- 
tion, or other similar uses. See Agreed Upon Statement of 
Facts, § 28. 


Perhaps the most offensive part of the amended MPCP 
is it compels Wisconsin citizens of varying religious faiths 


WURDE Reet Phe 


169a 


to support schools with their tax dollars that proselytize 
students and attempt to inculcate them with beliefs con- 
trary to their own. We do not object to the existence of 
parochial schools or that they attempt to spread their be- 
liefs through their schools. They just cannot do it with 
State tax dollars. 


Accordingly, the amended MPCP, by permitting reli- 
gious schools to Participate in the program, violates Ar- 


ticle I, sec. 18 of the Wisconsin Constitution because the 
primary effect of the amended MPCP is to benefit the 
religious missions of the elementary and secondary reli- 
gious schools and because it compels Wisconsin taxpayers 


to support places of worship without their consent. 


Private or Local Bill, Article IV, sec. 18 


Article IV, sec. 18 states, “(n)o private or local bill 
which may be passed by the legislature shall embrace more 
than one subject, and that shall be expressed in the title.” 
The purpose of this provision is: 


1.) (T)o encourage the legislature to devote its time 
to the state at large, its Primary responsibiilty; 


2.) to avoid the specter of favoritism and discrimina- 
tion, a potential which is inherent in laws of 
limited applicability; and 


3.) to alert the public through its elected representa- 
tives to the real nature and subject matter of 
legislation under consideration. 


Davis v. Grover, 166 Wis. 2d 501, 519, 480 N.W.2d 460 
(1992); Milwaukee Brewers Baseball Club v. Wisconsin 
Dep’t of Health and Social Services, 130 Wis. 2d 79, 107- 
08, 387 N.W.2d 254, 266 (1986). The legislature passed 
the amended MPCP as part of a voluminous budget bill. 
This reduces the legislature’s accountability to the public 
and leaves the legislation susceptible to being challenged 
under Article IV, sec. 18. See Davis v. Grover, 166 Wis. 


170a 


2d at 522. One of the principal concerns embodied in 
Article IV, sec. 18 is to avoid “internal logrolling” by the 
legislature. Id., at 519, citing, Brookfield v. Milwaukee 
Sewerage, 144 Wis. 2d 896, 426 N.W.2d 591 (1988) 
(footnote omitted). “Multisubject bills by their nature are 
subject to a greater susceptibility of smuggling and log- 
rolling.” Id. However, we realize that legislators may be 
forced by time constraints to pass legislation through 
multi-subject bills. Id. at 522. Therefore, this court has 
a duty to take a closer look at the legislation. 


Determining whether the amended MPCP constitutes a 
private or local bill involves a two part analysis: 1.) 
whether the process in which the bill was passed deserves 
a presumption of constitutionality, and 2.) whether the 
bill is private or local. 7d. at 520. If this court con- 
cludes that the amended MPCP constitutes a private or 
local bill, then Article ITV, sec. 18 will apply, and the 
legislation must be passed as a single subject bill with the 
title early indicating the subject matter. Id. 


First, this court must determine whether the process by 
which the amended MPCP was enacted deserves a pre- 
sumption of constitutionality. “Although a statute gen- 
erally enjoys a presumption of constitutionality, an excep- 
tion to the rule occurs when the behavior of the legisla- 
ture allegedly violates a law mandating the form in which 
bills must be enacted.” City of Oak Creek v. State Dep't 
of Natural Resources, 185 Wis. 2d 424, 437, 518 N.W. 
2d 276, 279 (Ct. App. 1994). See, Davis v. Grover, 
166 Wis. 2d at 520. In this case, the amended MPCP 
was passed as part of the 1995 Budget Act instead of 
as a single subject bill. 1995 Wis. Act 27, sec. 4002-09. 
Furthermore, the amended MPCP portion of the 1995 
Budget Act did not have a title clearly indicating its 
subject matter. Thus, the amended MPCP will only be 
presumed constitutional if evidence shows that the legis- 
lature adequately considered this legislation. 


17la 


The original MPCP was law since 1990, and received 
a lot of attenion. When the legislature decided to amend 
the program, public hearings were held in Milwaukee, 
Madison, Cedarburg, Portgage, and River Falls. In addi- 
tion, the Joint Committee on Finance debated the bill. 
Furthermore, the Assembly Committee on Urban Educa- 
tion and the Senate Education Committee held hearings 
regarding this legislation. This bill was also debated on 
the Assembly and Senate floor. Thus, this court con- 
cludes this legislation was not smuggled through the legis- 
lature by passing it as part of a voluminous budget bill. 


Second, this court must determine whether the amended 
MPCP constitutes a private or local bill. There are three 
categories of legislation that can be challenged under the 
local bill provision. First, legislation that is specific on its 
face as to particular people, places or things. See Davis, 
166 Wis. 2d at 524. Second, legislation that is not specific 
on its face, but involves prohibited classification under 
Article IV, sec. 31. Id. at 525. Third, legislation that is 
not specific on its face but creates a closed classification 
that limits it to a specific locality. Id. In this case, the 
amended MPCP falls within the third category, because it 
only applies to first class cities. At this time, Milwaukee 
is the only first class city in Wisconsin. Thus, we must 
analyze it under the test enunciated in Brookfield, supra. 


The elements of the Brookfield test are: 


1. The classification employed by the legislature 
must be based on substantial distinctions which 
make one class really different from another; 


2. The classification adopted must be germane to 
the purpose of the law; 


3. The classification must not be based on existing 
circumstances only. Instead, the classification 
must be subject to being open, such that other 
cities could join the class. 


172a 


4. When a law applies to a class, it must apply 
equally to all members of the class. 


5. The characteristics of each class should be so far 
different from those of the other classes so as to 
reasonably suggest at least the propriety, having 
regard to the public good, of substantially dif- 
ferent legislation. 


Brookfield, 144 Wis. 2d at 907-09. This test analyzes 
the substance of the legislation in order to determine 
whether the procedural requirements of the local bill 
provision apply. See Davis, 166 Wis. 2d at 526. In 
Davis, the court found that the original MPCP met all 
five elements. Id. at 527-37.° The parties dispute whether 
the program still meets the second element under the 
amended MPCP. No other element of the Brookfield test 
is at issue. 


The plaintiffs contend the adopted classification of 
first class cities is no longer germane to the purpose of 
the law. When determining whether the first class city 
classification was germane to the purpose of the law 
under the original MPCP, the trial court, the court of 
appeals, and the Wisconsin Supreme Court in Davis con- 
cluded that “the only reasonable inference to be drawn 
from the MPCP was that it was an experiment intended 
to address a perceived problem of inadequate educational 
opportunities for disadvantaged children.” Jd. at 530. 
The parties do not dispute that improving education in 
Wisconsin is of statewide importance, nor that a first 
class city is the best place to experiment with alternative 
forms of education. However, they do disagree on 
whether the amended MPCP still constitutes an experi- 


SIt is the rare opportunity to have a case to refer to which is 
almost entirely similar on the issues as Davis v. Grover. Davis is 
the predecessor of this case which, in part, addressed this issue. 
Hence, it behooves us to consider the Court’s analysis in that case 
to determine whether the primary bases for its holding is present 
in this case. 


Oe 
OT 


173a 


ment. In Davis, the court focused on two features of the 
program to indicate its experimental nature: the small 
size of the program and the extensive data compilation 
and reporting requirements. Jd. at 533-34. The plaintiffs 
contend the program no longer constitutes an experiment, 
because the amended MPCP substantially expanded the 
size of the program and severely reduced the data com- 
pilation requirements. 


First, the Davis court stated, “the program is not an 
abandonment of the public school system. Rather, the 
MPCP would affect at most only 1 percent of the stu- 
dents in the MPS, giving the program a very small 
window of opportunity to test the effectiveness of an 
alternative to the MPS.” Id. at 533. Since Davis v. 
Grover was decided, the program has expanded to in- 
clude up to 15% of the students in the MPS. Allowing 
15,000 students to participate in this program can no 
longer be considered “a small window of opportunity.” 
Nonetheless, expanding the number of participating stu- 
dents and schools increases the state’s ability to assess the 
effectiveness of educational choice and to evaluate dif- 
ferent models. “The success of the program is dependent 
upon the participation of numerous and diverse non- 
sectarian private schools such that the fate of the pro- 
gram does not rest on the operations of one or a few 
schools.” Id. at 534. 


Second, the Davis court found that “the MPCP creates 
an extensive data compilation and reporting process 
which the state can use to measure the effects of choice 
and competition in education.” Jd. at 533. However, 
the amended MPCP eliminated sec. 119:23(5)(d), 
Stats., which required the superintendent. to prepare and 
circulate “a report comparing the academic achievement, 
daily attendance record, percentage of dropouts, percent- 
age of pupils suspended and expelled and parental in- 
volvement activities” of pupils participating in the MPCP 
with those attending public schools in Milwaukee. 1995 


174a 


Wis. Act 27, sec. 4007m. The amended MPCP also elimi- 
nated sec. 119.23(9)(a), Stats., which author’zed the 
superintendent to “conduct one or more financial or per- 
formance evaluations, audits, or both.” Section 119.23(9), 
Stats., is the only remaining evaluative component of the 
MPCP. This statute requires the legislative audit bureau 
to conduct a financial and performance evaluation audit 
of the program by January 15, 2000 to submit to the 
chief clerk of each house of the legislature. Id. 


Without regular evaluation of the MPCP, this court 
questions its experimental nature. Experiments are exe- 
cuted to test a certain hypothesis. However, how can the 
state test the hypothesis that choice improves education 
without providing for routine and systematic data collec- 
tion and compilation? No provision in the amended 
MPCP is provided to enable the state to make meaningful 
comparisons between students who participate in this pro- 
gram with those attending public schools. The primary 
provision for such data compilation and comparison was 
repealed by the amended MPCP, sec. 119.23(5)(d), Stats. 
Thus, this court finds that the amended MPCP is no longer 
an experiment. The legislature, by repealing sec. 119.23 
(5)(d), Stats., eliminated one of the two principal bases 
upon which the Davis court relied in finding the Original 
MPCP constitutional under Article IV, sec. 18. 


Furthermore, this court is not aware of any other fea- 
tures of the amended MPCP that make the first class city 
classification germane to the purpose of the law. We agree 
that a first class city is the best place to test experimental 
forms of education. However, if the program is no longer 
experimental, it will not benefit children outside of Mil- 
waukee. The existence of so many private schools in the 
City of Milwaukee becomes completely irrelevant if no 
significant data compilation system exists. Thus, it no 
longer serves a statewide purpose, and is no longer “ger- 


Sap es aS 


Re sh CORIO 


175a 


mane to the purpose of the law.” Therefore, this court 
finds the amended MPCP is a local or private bill enacted 
in violation of Article IV, sec. 18. 


Public Purpose Doctrine 


Another challenge to the constitutionality of the amended 
MPCP involves the application of the public purpose doc- 
trine. The public purpose doctrine states that public ex- 
penditures may be made only for public purposes. “An 
expenditure of public funds for other than a public pur- 
pose would be abhorrent to the constitution of Wisconsin.” 
See State ex rel. Warren v. Nusbaum, 59 Wis. 2d at 414. 
Although the public purpose doctrine is not an express 
provision of the Wisconsin Constitution, it is firmly ac- 
cepted as a basic constitutional tenet. See State ex rel. 
Bowman v. Barczak, 34 Wis. 2d 57, 62, 148 N.W.2d 683, 
684 (1967). 


Several theories exist regarding the origin of the public 
purpose doctrine. The Wisconsin Supreme Court has 
attributed its development to the due process and equal 
protection clauses of the state and federal constitutions. 
See State ex rel. Wisconsin Dev. Authority v. Dammann, 
228 Wis. 147, 175, 277 N.W. 278 (1938). In Heimer! 
v. Ozaukee County, 256 Wis. 151, 158, 40 N.W.2d 
564, 567 (1949), the Wisconsin Supreme Court attrib- 
uted it to U.S. Const. art. IV, sec. 4 stating, “(t)axation 
for the private purpose is prohibited by the clause of the 
federal constitution that guarantees to every state a Re- 
publican form of government, as such a form of govern- 
ment forbids the raising of taxes for anything but a pub- 
lic purpose.” Furthermore, Wis. Const. Article VIII, 
sec. 2, provides that no money shall be paid out of the 
treasury except in pursuance of an appropriation by law. 
See State ex rel. La Follette v. Reuter, 33 Wis. 2d 384, 
147 N.W.2d 304 (1967). Finally, others trace the doc- 
trine to a “judicial articulation of the belief that govern- 
mental power should be used for the benefit of the entire 


176a 


community.” See Lewis R. Mills, The Public Purpose 
Doctrine in Wisconsin, 1957 Wis. L. Rev. 40, 40. 


The difficulty arises in determining what constitutes a 
valid public purpose. “(W)hat constitutes a public pur- 
pose is in the first instance a question for the legislature 
to determine and its opinion should be given great 
weight.” State ex rel. Warren v. Reuter, 44 Wis. 2d at 
212. The court must recognize that “the hierarchy of 
community values is best determined by the will of the 
electorate” and that “legislative decisions are more repre- 
sentative of popular opinion because individuals have 
greater access to their legislative representatives.” Barc- 
zak, 34 Wis. 2d at 65. Thus, the court “will not inter- 
fere unless at first blush the act appears to be so obvi- 
ously designed in all its principal parts to benefit private 
persons and so indirectly or remotely to affect the public 
interest that it constitutes the taking of property of the 
taxpayers for private use.” Id. at 64. 


Furthermore, what constitutes a public purpose is not 
a static concept. Id. Over time, the scope of activities 
which may be classified as a valid public purpose has 
broadened. See State ex rel. Hammermill Paper Co. v. 
La Plante, 58 Wis. 2d 32, 54, 205 N.W.2d 784, 797 
(1973). In La Plante, the following cases were cited to 
illustrate the steady growth of the public purpose doctrine 
under the decisions of the Wisconsin Supreme Court. 


In State ex rel. Bowman v. Barczak, supra, indus- 
trial development through agencies and bond issues, 
pursuant to sec. 59.071, Stats., was determined to 
be a valid constitutional enactment as it related to a 
declaration of public purpose. In West Allis v. Mil- 
waukee County, 39 Wis. 2d 356, 159 N.W.2d 36 
(1968), construction of incinerators and waste dis- 
posal facilities was considered a public purpose. In 
State ex rel. Warren v. Reuter, supra, financial aid 
to Marquette School of Medicine, a private non- 


EE 


PEE VEE FPR REN Sv 


177a 


profit corporation, was upheld upon the premise that 
public health is a public purpose. 


Id, at 55. 


The amended MPCP is similar to the original MPCP 
which was challenged in Davis v. Grover. In Davis, the 
Wisconsin Supreme Court held that the original MPCP 
did not violate the public purpose doctrine. Id. at 546. 
Thus, this court will begin by looking at the holding in 
that case. 


In Davis, the court stated, “(n)o party disputes that 
education constitutes a valid public purpose, nor that 
private schools many be employed to further that pur- 
pose.” See id. at 541. Thus, the only dispute between 
the parties was whether adequate controls existed to en- 
sure the attainment of the public purpose. 


The Wisconsin Supreme Court found that adequate 
controls did exist under the original MPCP. The court 
held that “(i)t simply does not matter how the school 
spends the money so long as it gives the participating 
student an education that complies with sec. 118.165, 
Stats., in return for the money.” Jd. at 542. Section 
118.165, Stats., requires private schools to: 


1.) be organized to primarily provide private or 
religious-based education; 


2.) be privately controlled; 


3.) provide at least 875 hours of instruction each 
school year; 


4.) provide a sequentially progressive curriculum of 
fundamental instructions in reading, language 
arts, mathematics, social studies, science, and 


health; 
5.) not be operated or instituted for the purpose of 


of avoiding or circumventing compulsory school 
attendance; and 


178a 


6.) have pupils return home not less than two 
months of each year unless the institution is also 
licensed as a child welfare agency. 


The court further cited the additional reporting require- 
ments included in the program. They noted that the 
state superintendent had to prepare an annual report for 
the legislature which compared data on children who par- 
ticipated in the original MPCP with students who at- 
tended Milwaukee public schools in the following areas: 
academic achievement, daily attendance, percentage of 
dropouts, and percentage of pupils suspended and ex- 
pelled. Id. at 544. Detailed reports and evaluations were 
also generated by the program. The original MPCP 
authorized the state superintendent and required the Leg- 
islative Audit Bureau to conduct financial and perform- 
ance audits on the program. /d. 


Finally, the court recognized the importance of pa- 
rental choice in controlling the education received by 
students participating in the original program. The court 
stated: 


Parents generally know their children better than 
anyone. The program allows participating parents 
to choose a school with an environment that matches 
their child’s personality, with a curriculum that 
matches their child’s interest and needs, and with a 
location that is convenient. If the private school 
does not meet the parents’ expectations, the parents 
may remove the child from the school and go else- 
where. In this way, parental choice preserves ac- 
countability for the best interests of the children. 
Id. 


In Davis, the court compared the original MPCP to the 
Situation in Reuter. Id. at 542. In that case, the court 
upheld an appropriation of public funds to Marquette Med- 
ical School for the purpose of providing quality medical 
education in Wisconsin. Id. However, Marquette Medical 
School was required to use the funds for “medical educa- 
tion, teaching, and research.” Jd. Under the original 


179a 


MPCP, sec. 119.23 Stats., did not provide any require- 
ments on how the funds provided to the participating 
schools had to be spent. Jd. Nonetheless, the court found 
there was adequate control since the schools were required 
to provide an education complying with the statutory re- 
quirements for private schools. /d. The court also ad- 
dressed the challenge that the Marquette Medical School 
was “accredited by an independent national organization 
as well as federal and state agencies” while schools par- 
ticipating in the original MPCP were not required to dem- 
onstrate that they were providing quality education. /d. 
The court found that the requirements of the program 
along with parental choice provided adequate controls. 


In Davis, the court used a two step analysis to deter- 
mine whether the public purpose doctrine had been vio- 
lated: 1.) whether there was a valid public purpose and 
2.) if so, whether there was adequate control and account- 
ability to ensure the attainment of the public purpose. /d. 
at 540-45. This court will begin by applying the same 
analysis to the amended MPCP. 


First, “(n)o party disputes that education constitutes a 
valid public purpose, nor that private schools may be em- 
ployed to further that purpose.” /d. at 541. The only rele- 
vant change under the amended MPCP is the addition of 
sectarian schools. Nonetheless, all participating children 
still receive an education. “We cannot take the short 
rather than the long view of the problem because a private 
school is used as a means to attain the end.” State ex rel. 
Warren, 44 Wis. 2d at 214. Indeed, expanding the MPCP 
enhances this public purpose by allowing more students to 
participate in the program. 


Secondly, this court must consider whether there are 
adequate controls to ensure the attainment of the public 
purpose. The amended MPCP eliminated some of the re- 
porting requirements. However, “(o)nly such control and 
accountability as is reasonably necessary under the circum- 


180a 


stances to attain the public purpose is required.” Jd. at 
216. In Davis, the court found that reasonable controls 
existed. This court finds that adequate controls still exist. 


Under the original MPCP, there were no express limita- 
tions on the use of the funds paid to the participating 
schools. The Wisconsin Supreme Court held that “(i)t 
simply does not matter how the school spends the money 
so long as it gives the participating student an education 
that complies with sec. 118.165, Stats., in return for the 
money.” Davis at 542. Under the amended MPCP the 
participating schools must still comply with sec. 118.165, 
Stats., which ensures a sequentially progressive curriculum. 
Ensuring a sequentially progressive curriculum ensures the 
attainment of the public purpose. 


In addition, the amended MPCP still contains several 
reporting requirements. The amended MPCP eliminated 
sec. 119.23(5)(d) Stats., requiring the state superintend- 
ent to submit an annual report to the legislature compar- 
ing students participating in thé program with students 
attending Milwaukee public schools. It also eliminated the 
state superintendent’s authority to conduct financial and 
performance audits under sec. 119.23(9)(a) Stats. How- 
ever, these reporting requirements are unnecessary to en- 
sure the attainment of the public purpose. The court’s 
only concern is that the controls ensure that participating 
students receive an education. Participating schools must 
still submit to an annual independent financial audit under 
sec. 119.23(7), Stats. Furthermore, the legislative audit 
bureau must submit a fiancial and performance audit of 
the program to the chief clerk of each house of the legisla- 
ture, for distrbution to appropriate standing committees, 
by January 15, 2000 under sec. 119.23(9)), Stats. 


Finally, parental choice still exists. The theory behind 
this program is that “less bureaucracy coupled with paren- 
tal choice improves educational quality” since “parental 
choice preserves accountability for the best interests of the 


18la 


children.” Davis at 544-45. Wisconsin recognizes that 
parents are “the persons under natural conditions having 
the most effective motives and inclinations and being in 
the best position and under the strongest obligation to give 
to such children proper nurture, education, and training.” 
See Wisconsin Indus. Sch. for Girls vy. Clark County, 103 
Wis. 651, 668-69, 79 N.W. 422, 428 (1899). 


Under the analysis used in Davis, education still consti- 
tutes a valid public purpose, and adequate controls still 
exist. However, there are times when using the traditional 
framework is unnecessary. The amended MPCP uses both 
sectarian and nonsectarian schools to educate participating 
students. Clearly the legislature may use private institu- 
tions to achieve a public purpose. Nonetheless, the reli- 
gious education provided by the participating sectarian 
schools does not serve a valid public purpose. Using sec- 
tarian schools to provide religious education with public 
money so blatantly violates the public purpose doctrine 
that it requires this court to go outside the traditional 
analysis. 


The legislature has wide discretion to determine what 
constitutes a public purpose, but the constitution clearly 
calls for a separation of church and state. “(T)he con- 
stitutional question remains as to how far the court should 
go in deferring to the conclusions of the legislature.” 
Barczak, 34 Wis. 2d at 65. When leigslation is challenged, 
the court has an “unavoidable burden under the constitu- 
tion to examine such legislation and to assess its realistic 
operations.” Id. at 65-66, quoting David Jeffrey Co. v. 
Milwaukee, 267 Wis. 559, 578, 66 N.W.2d 362 (1954). 
“Although the legislative declarations are entitled to great 
weight, we may not blindly accept at full value even the 
most elaborate prefatory expressions concerning commu- 
nity need, economic impact, or public purpose.” Jd. 


Normally “(w)e are not concerned with the ‘wisdom, 
merits or practicability of the legislature’s enactment.’ ” 


182a 


See Millers Nat'l Ins. Co. v. City of Milwaukee, 184 Wis. 
2d 155, 175, 516 N.W.2d 376, 383 quoting Hopper v. 
Madison, 79 Wis. 2d 120, 128-29, 256 N.W.2d 139 
(1977). In reviewing a “legislative expenditure of public 
funds, there is a strong presumption that a legislature’s 
acts are constitutional, and it is the duty of this court, if 
possible, to construe a legislative enactment as to find it in 
harmony with constitutional principals.” See Id. However, 
the legislature exceeded their role by funding religious edu- 
cation with public funds, and this court can not defer to 
the legislature’s expression of public purpose. 


Sectarian schools make up the majority of the partici- 
pating schools in the amended MPCP. During the 1994- 
95 school year, 84% of the participating students at- 
tended religious schools. Education in sectarian schools 
contain two components: 1.) secular instruction and 
2.) religious instruction. The mission statements of the 
religious schoools clearly indicate that their function is to 
promote religion. Secular instruction in these schools in- 
tertwines worship and religious indoctrination designed 
to advance their religious goals. Furthermore, most schools 
receive more than they charge for tuition, and there are no 
restrictions on how the schools spend this money. Thus, 


public money inevitably goes to fund religious education 
and activities. 


The public purpose doctrine clearly states that public 
funds may “only” be used for public purposes. Granted, 
the public purpose doctrine does not include an establish- 
ment clause component. Nevertheless, there are no cases 


upholding government funding of religious activities as a 
valid public purpose. 


The test to be applied in determining whether a 
particular agency may be employed by the state or 
some particular subdivision thereof by legislative au- 
thorization, to perform any particular work, is not 
whether the agency is public, but whether the purpose 


183a 


is public within the legitimate function of our consti- 
tutional government. If the purpose be public and 
constitutional, and the agency be an appropriate 
means to accomplish it, and not expressly or by neces- 
sary implication prohibited by state or national con- 
stitution, its employment, under reasonable regula- 
tions for control and accountability to secure public 
interests, is legitimate and constitutional. 


See Wisconsin Indus. Sch. for Girls, 103 Wis. at 667. 
The fact that the establishment clause and Article 1, sec. 
18 make funding religious activities with public money 
unconstitutional leads to the conclusion that sectarian 
schools providing religious education can not be used to 
achieve the public purpose of educating students. 


Using the traditional analysis, the amended MPCP re- 
mains constitutional under the public purpose doctrine. 
This holds true for nonsectarian schools. However, reli- 
gious education does not constitute a valid public purpose. 
Thus, the use of sectarian schools is unconstitutional under 
the public purpose doctrine. 


Uniformity Clause, Article X, sec. 3 


The amended MPCP has been challenged as violating 
the uniformity clause, Article X, sec. 3. The uniformity 
clause provides: 


(t)he legislature shall provide by law for the es- 
tablishment of district schools, which shall be as 
nearly uniform as practicable; and such schools shall 
be free and without charge for tuition to all children 
between the ages of 4 and 20 years; and no sectarian 
instruction shall be allowed therein; imt the legisla- 
ture by law may, for the purpose of religious instruc- 
tion outside the district schools, authorize the release 
of students during regular school hours. 


184a 


Article X, sec. 3. This court will apply a two step analy- 
sis to determine whether the uniformity clause has been 
violated: 1.) whether participating schools are district 
schools, and 2.) if so, whether the character of instruction 
is uniform. 


The first issue revolves around the definition of “district 
school.” The Wisconsin Constitution does not specifically 
define the term “district school.” We can look to the in- 
tent of the framers of the Wisconsin Constitution, but de- 
fining what constitutes a district school did not appear to 
be their primary concern. 


An examination of the debates in the conventions 
that framed our present constitution and the constitu- 
tion of 1846 (which contained a similar provision) 
discloses that the members of those conventions, when 
they were framing the article relating to schools, were 
concerned, not with the method of forming school 
districts, but with the character of instruction that 
should be given in those schools after the districts 
were formed,—with the training that these schools 
should give to the future citizens of Wisconsin. 


Viewing the terms of this constitutional provision 
in light of its express terms as well as of the purpose 
which actuated those who drafted it, we conclude that 
the requirement as to uniformity applies to the dis- 
tricts after they are formed,—to the character of the 
instruction given,—rather than to the means by which 
they are established and their boundaries fixed. 


See Kukor v. Grover, 148 Wis. 2d 469, 486, 436 N.W. 
2d 568, 575 (1989), citing, State ex rel. Zilisch v. Auer, 
197 Wis. 284, 289-90, 223 N.W. 123 (1928). 


Furthermore, in Davis, the court clearly rejected the 
theory that any school that accepts public money be- 
comes a district school. Davis, at 539-40. The court 
stated: 


185a 


This theory flies directly in the face of past decisions 
by this court. In State ex rel. Warren v. Reuter, 44 
Wis. 2d 201, 170 N.W.2d 790 (1969), we held that 
the appropriation of public funds to a private entity 
need only be accompanied by such controls as are 
necessary to fulfill the public purpose required. De- 
pending on the circumstances, these controls do not 
necessarily have to be the same as those regulating 
similar public agencies. 


Id. Thus, participating schools are not automatically 
district schools. 


The amended MPCP is similar to the original MPCP 
which was challenged in Davis. Id. In that case, the 
Wisconsin Supreme Court found that the original MPCP 
did not violate the uniformity clause. Jd. at 539. This 
court will look at the analysis used in that case. 


In Davis, the court held that participating schools were 
not required to provide uniform education, because they 
did not constitute district schools for the purpose of the 
uniformity clause. Jd. at 538. The court noted that the 
original MPCP unambiguously referred to “private 
schools” which has a specific statutory meaning. Id. at 
538. Section 15.001(3r), Stats., defines a private school 
as “an institution with a private educational program 
that meets all of the criteria under s. 118.165(1) or is 
determined to be a private school by the state superin- 
tendent under s. 118.167.” In deciding this issue, the 
Wisconsin Supreme Court stated, “(w)e assume that the 
legislature was aware of this statutory meaning and in- 
tended to use ‘private school’. . . as a stautory term of 
art.” Davis at 538. 


Furthermore, in Davis, the court determined that the 
original MPCP did not deprive any student of the oppor- 
tunity to attend a public school providing uniform edu- 
cation. Jd. at 538. They cited to the fact that all stu- 
dents participating in the program have the option to 


186a 


“withdraw at any time and return to a public school.” 
Id. The court concluded that the original MPCP merely 
reflected “a legislative desire to do more than that which 
is constitutionally mandated.” Id. 


The court has clearly established the principal that the 
uniformity clause creates a floor and not a ceiling for 
legislative action. See, e.g., Zweifel v. Joint Dist. No. 1, 
Belleville, 76 Wis. 2d 648, 251 N.W.2d 822 (1977). In 
Davis, the court looked at the following cases to illus- 
trate this point. Davis at 538. In Comstock v. Joint 
Sch. Dist. No. 1, 65 Wis. 631, 636-37, 27 N.W. 829 
(1888), the Wisconsin Supreme Court found a statute 
allowing school districts to admit nonresident school chil- 
dren constitutional under the uniformity clause. The 
court declared, “when the legislature has provided for 
each such child the privileges of a district school, which 
he or she may freely enjoy, the constitutional require- 
ment in that behalf is complied with.” Jd. Furthermore, 
in Kukor, the Wisconsin Supreme Court found that a 
legislative formula used to determine the amount of state 
aid to public school districts was constitutional under the 
uniformity clause, because the students had the oppor- 
tunity to attend public school providing uniform instruc- 
tion. Kukor at 496-97. 


This court must follow the reasoning applied in Davis, 
unless the amended MPCP can be clearly ditinguished 
from the original MPCP. However, little has changed 
from the original MPCP with respect to the uniformity 
clause. 


Choice opponents argue the schools participating in 
the amended MPCP no longer constitute district schools 
since the legislature significantly expanded the program. 
Instead of being a small, experimental program, choice 
opponents claim that private schools are becoming a sig- 
nificant and integral part of the required system. That 
may be so, but it is irrelevant under the Davis analysis. 
The legislature still refers to the participating schools as 
“private schools” which still has a specific statutory defi- 


187a 


nition. The amended MPCP does not change this lan- 
guage in any way. Even where private schools are cre- 
ated by an act of the legislature, they remain private 
as long as the institution retains control. See Attorney 
General ex rel. Saunders v. Albion Academy & Normal 
Institute, 52 Wis. 469, 9 N.W. 391 (1881). Under the 
amended MPCP, ownership and control has not been 
transferred to the state. 


In addition, students may still withdraw at any time 
to return to public schools. The uniformity clause does 
not require the legislature to ensure that all children in 
Wisconsin actually receive a free uniform basic educa- 
tion. Davis at 538. The legislature must merely ensure 
that all children have the opportunity to receive a free 
uniform basic education, and the amended MPCP in no 
way prevents this. Jd. Once the legislature meets this 
requirement, they are free to add to the basic foundation 
required by the uniformity clause. Jd. at 539. 


Choice opponents further cite to the fact that private 
schools may now receive all of their funding through 
public money just like any other district school that must 
comply with the uniformity clause. As the program was 
originally enacted, no school could receive more than 
half of its funds from the state. This restriction was 
repealed. Nonetheless, the notion that schools receiving 
public money are transformed into district schools was 
clearly rejected in Davis. Id. 


The 1995 amendments to the MPCP do not change 
the analysis applied to the original MPCP. Thus, this 
court finds no compelling reason to depart from the hold- 
ing in Davis. Schools participating in the amended 
MPCP are not district schools so they are not required 
to provide uniform education nor restricted from pro- 
viding sectarian instruction. Therefore, the amended 
MPCP does not violate the uniformiy clause, Article X, 
sec. 3. 


188a 


CONCLUSION 


For the above reasons this court holds that the 
amended MPCP violates Article I, sec. 18, Article IV, 
sec. 18, and the private purpose doctrine as applied to 
the sectarian schools. The amended MPCP is constitu- 
tional under Article X, sec. 3 and the private purpose 
doctrine as applied to nonsectarian schools. Summary 
judgment is granted in favor of plaintiffs and the State 
of Wisconsin is ordered to terminate the amended Mil- 
waukee Parental Choice Program accordingly. By stipu- 
lation this order is stayed until the close of the 1996-97 
school year. 


Dated: January 15, 1997. 
By THE CourRT: 
/s/ Paul B. Higginbotham 
PAUL B. HIGGINBOTHAM 
Circuit Judge

assistance of Jennifer Hanneman

PER CURIAM. The court is equally divided. Chief 
Justice Day, Justice Abrahamson and Jusiice Bablitch be- 
lieve that the amendments to the Milwaukee Parental 
Choice Program (MPCP) are contrary to art. 1, § 18 and 
art. X, § 3 of the Wisconsin Constitution.' 


Justice Steinmetz, Justice Wilcox and Justice Geske be- 
lieve, based both upon the legislation before us and the 
stipulated facts, that respondents have not met the burden 
to prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the legislative 
amendments to MPCP violate the Establishment Clause 
of the First Amendment to the U.S. Const., art. I, § 18 
of the Wis. Const., art. X, § 3 of the Wis. Const. or Wis- 
consin’s Public Purpose Doctrine.” 


1 Because these three justices would hold the expanded MPCP 
program unconstitutional under the Wisconsin Constitution, they 
would not reach the issue of whether it should also be found un- 
constitutional under the United States Constitution. 


2 The original program withstood challenges brought on a variety 
of state constitutional bases in Davis v. Grover, 166 Wis. 2d 501, 
512, 480 N.W.2d 460 (1992), in part because the majority of this 
court concluded that the program was experimental in nature and 
served to advance the goal of improving “the quality of education 
in Wisconsin for children of low-income families.” Justices Stein- 


19la 


A majority of the participating judges must agree on a 
particular point for it to be considered the opinion of the 
court. State v. Elam, 195 Wis. 2d 683, 685, 538 N.W.2d 
249 (1995). Conversely, when the court splits evenly, as 
is the case here, the court would ordinarily affirm the 
court of appeals’ decision if the case was before the court 
on appeal, or remand to the court of appeals for further 
proceedings if the case was before the court on a bypass 
or certification. Id. at 684-85. This case, however, is 
not before the court on appeal, bypass or certification but 
rather as an original action pursuant to a petition for 
removal from the Dane County Circuit Court. 


Accordingly, the stay of proceedings pending in the 
Dane County Circuit Court is lifted; the preliminary in- 
junction enjoining all portions of the amended MPCP is 
continued until further order of the Dane County Circuit 
Court; and the original action proceeding pending before 
this court is dismissed without prejudice. 


ANN WALSH BRADLEY, J., did not participate. 


metz, Wilcox and Geske believe that while the legislature has chosen 
to expand the program in the amendments challenged here, the 
program is still limited and experimental and, under the stipulated 
facts, remains inoffensive to the dictates of the United States Con- 
stitution, the Wisconsin Constitution and the Wisconsin Public 
Purpose Doctrine.

APPENDIX E 


WEST’S WISCONSIN STATUTES ANNOTATED 
SCHOOLS 
CHAPTER 119. FIRST CLASS 
CITY SCHOOL SYSTEM 


Current through 1995 Act 135, published 1/5/96 
119.23. Milwaukee parental choice program 


(1) In this section, “membership” has the meaning given 
in s. 121.004(5). 


(2)(a) Subject to par. (b), beginning in the 1990-91 
school year, any pupil in grades kindergarten to 12 who 
resides within the city may attend, at no charge, any pri- 
vate school located in the city if all of the following apply: 


1. The pupil is a member of a family that has a total 
family income that does not exceed an amount equal 
to 1.75 times the poverty level determined in accordance 
with criteria established by the director of the federal 
office of management and budget. 


2. In the previous school year the pupil was enrolled in 
the school district operating under this chapter, was at- 
tending a private school under this section, was enrolled 
in grades kindergarten to 3 in a private school located in 
the city other than under this section or was not enrolled 
in school. 


3. The private school notified the department of its intent 
to participate in the program under this section by May 1 
of the previous school year. The notice shall specify the 
number of pupils participating in the program under this 
section for which the school has space. 


4. The private school complies with 42 USC 2000d. 


5. The private school meets all health and safety laws 
or codes that apply to public schools. 


193a 


(b) In the 1995-96 school year, no more than 7% of the 
school district’s membership may attend private schools 
under this section. Beginning in the 1996-97 school year, 
no more than 15% of the school district’s membership may 
attend private schools under this section. 


(3)(a) The pupil or the pupil’s parent or guardian shall 
submit an application, on a form provided by the depart- 
ment, to the participating private school that the pupil 
wishes to attend. Within 60 days after receiving the appli- 
cation, the private school shall notify the applicant, in 
writing, whether the application has been accepted. The 
department shall ensure that the private school determines 
which pupils to accept on a random basis, except that the 
private school may give preference in accepting applica- 
tions to siblings of pupils accepted on a random basis. 


(b) If the private school rejects an applicant because it 
has too few available spaces, the pupil may transfer his 
or her application to a participating private school that 
has space available. 


(4) Upon receipt from the pupil’s parent or guardian of 
proof of the pupil’s enrollment in the private school, the 
department shall pay to the parent or guardian, from the 
appropriaton under s. 20.255(2) (fu), an amount equal to 
the total amount to which the school district is entitled 
under s. 121.08 divided by the school district membership, 
or an amount equal to the private school’s operating and 
debt service cost per pupil that is related to educational 
programming, as determined by the department, whichever 
is less. The department shall pay 25% of the total amount 
in September, 25% in November, 25% in February and 
25% in May. The department shall send the check to 
the private school. The parent or guardian shall restric- 
tively endorse the check for the use of the private school. 


(5) The department shall: 


194a 


(a) Annually reduce the aid paid to the board under 
s. 121.08 by an amount determined as follows: — 


1. Divide the total amount to which the school district 


is entitled under s. 121.08 by the school district member- 
ship. 


2. Multiply the quotient under subd. 1 by the number of 
pupils attending private schools under this section. 


(b) Ensure that aid paid to other school districts under 
s. 121.98 is neither reduced nor increased as a result of 
the payments under sub. (4) or the reduction in aid to the 
board under par. (a) and that the amount of the aid re- 
duction under par. (a) lapses to the general fund. 


(c) Ensure that pupils and parents and guardians of 
pupils who reside in the city are informed annually of 
the private schools participating in the program under this 
section. 


(d) Repealed by 1995 Act 27, § 4007m, eff. July 29, 
1995. 


(6) The board shall provide transportation to pupils at- 
tending a private school under this section if required 
under s. 121.54 and may claim transportation aid under 
s. 121.58 for pupils so transported. 


(7)(a) Each private school participating in the program 
under this section shall meet at least one of the following 
standards: 


1. At least 70% of the pupils in the program advance one 
grade level each year. 


2. The private school’s average attendance rate for the 
pupils in the program is at least 90%. 


3. At least 80% of the pupils in the program demon- 
strate significant academic progress. 


195a 


4. At least 70% of the families of pupils in the program 
meet parent involvement criteria established by the private 
school. 


(am) Each private school participating in the program 
under this section is subject to uniform financial account- 
ing standards established by the department and annually 
shall submit to the department an independent financial 
audit of the private school. 


(b) The department shall monitor the performance of the 
pupils attending private schools under this section. If the 
department determines in any school year that the private 
school is not meeting at least one of the standards under 
par. (a), that private school may not participate in the 
program under this section in the following school year. 


(c) A private school may not require a pupil attending 
the private school under this section to participate in any 
religious activity if the pupil’s parent or guardian submits 
to the pupil’s teacher or the private school’s principal a 
written request that the pupil be exempt from such ac- 
tivities. 

(8) There is created a pupil assignment council com- 
posed of one representative from each private school par- 
ticipating in the program under this section. Annually by 
June 30, the council shall make recommendations to the 
participating private schools to achieve, to the extent pos- 
sible, a balanced representation of pupils participating in 
the program under this section. 


(9) The legislative audit bureau shall perform a financial 
and performance evaluation audit on the program under 
this section. The bureau shall submit copies of the audit 
report to the chief clerk of each house of the legislature 
for distributon to the appropriate standing committees 
under s. 13.172(3) by January 15, 2000.
